# LLP 2000.000: Snapback 3 — keep the core, flip two substrates, start at hub scale

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Draft
**Role:** Second opinion on LLP 2000; since 2026-09-12 also the design brief Snapback 4 builds (LLP 3000)
**Systems:** Snapback 3 product architecture, bounded computation, client data model, client API vocabulary, language and tooling, effects runtime, verification (simulation), performance budgets, process
**Author:** Claude Fable 5.1, at Charlie Cheever's request
**Date:** 2026-09-12
**Revised:** 2026-09-12 (adopted as the Snapback 4 design brief by LLP 3000 decision 3); 2026-09-12 (initial)
**Related:** [LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md) (parent), [LLP 3000](./3000-snapback4.explainer.md) (the Snapback 4 root that builds this brief), LLP 0000, [LLP 0329](./0329-agent-native-rebuild-diagnosis.research.md), [LLP 0329.014.004](./0329.014.004-bounded-data-programs-v1-design.rfc.md), [LLP 0329.016](./0329.016-agentic-dev-loop-from-scratch.rfc.md), [LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md), [LLP 1001](./1001-query-engine.spec.md), [LLP 1002](./1002-dev-loop.spec.md), [LLP 1012](./1012-local-first-v1.spec.md), [LLP 1018.003](./1018.003-truthful-and-composed-client.decision.md), [LLP 1018.008](./1018.008-elegance-after-the-corrections.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.007](./1018.008.007-affordable-offline-acquisition.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.008](./1018.008.008-locality-of-application-changes.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.009](./1018.008.009-residual-ergonomic-gaps.research.md), [LLP 1019](./1019-derived-delivery-custody.rfc.md), [LLP 1020](./1020-day-two-evolution-and-legible-ceilings.rfc.md), [LLP 1023](./1023-clique-builder-jump.rfc.md), [LLP 1024](./1024-single-lane-delivered-search.rfc.md), [LLP 1025](./1025-digest-final-emission-at-hub-scale.research.md), `rules/RULES.md`, `rules/NOT-DOING.md`, `benchmarks/runs/2026-09-05-clique-full-3x3/holistic-scores.md`

## 1. Summary

LLP 2000 is right that the whole application is the unit of design, right
that ownership comes before abstraction, and right to ask for deterministic
simulation and an early real deployment. Its weakness is that it treats the
two structural choices that produced Snapback 2's costs as open research
questions, and then proposes a first experiment at exactly the scale where
those costs are invisible. This document takes a position on both, names
what to keep from Snapback 2 verbatim, and rewrites the first slice so it
can fail for the right reasons.

The position, in one paragraph. Snapback 2's *core* is excellent and should
be copied: the rule language and its uniformity analysis, the structural-fact
family, one data language, typed states with no silence, refusals that paste
a rewrite, personas, the successor classifier, the derived per-recipient
log, the loop verbs and their sub-second budgets. Two *substrates* under
that core are where the record shows the hours went, and both should be
flipped rather than re-investigated: (1) the static certificate should keep
certifying **authorization and shape** and stop pricing **bytes and steps**,
which move to a deterministic runtime meter with honest `capped`/abort
outcomes; and (2) the client should hold **a replica of the viewer's declared
partition** — the local-first shape every shipped sync engine and Snapback
2's own `deliver` log converge on — instead of a per-query result cache
proven by receipts. Those two flips dissolve most of the machinery that
leaked into the application (receipts, coverage, halos, validators, grants,
ID slices, drains, `withHistory`, follower inboxes, per-table retention
words) and turn the 28-second acquisition into a bulk stream whose floor is
the device's write speed. The first slice should be built on the **hub
fixture** (1,000 memberships, 500 follows, 100,000 messages), with the
day-two change designed before the day-one screen, a native client in the
first slice, and a deterministic simulator as the primary test harness.

Three claims this document makes that LLP 2000 does not:

1. **The costs have two named causes, and both are decisions, not
   mysteries.** Byte-priced static certification and the receipt-proven
   result cache account for every ergonomic residual in LLP 1018.008.009
   except documentation. §2 shows the mapping.
2. **The first experiment must include the hub.** Snapback 2 was "elegant on
   day one and hostile on day two" (LLP 1020) and broke at 1,000 memberships
   (LLPs 1023, 1024, 1025). A two-principal slice will pass and teach nothing.
3. **The process is part of the product.** Seventy-one LLPs, over two
   thousand commits, and seven ceiling raises in fourteen days produced a
   sound system nobody can hold in their head. Snapback 3 needs one owner on
   the core, a simulator instead of a test matrix, and budgets anchored to
   competitors so that raising one means admitting a loss.

## 2. What the record says, read as causes

The evidence LLP 2000 §2 cites is right; its reading is too gentle. Every
row below is a Snapback 2 cost recorded in the corpus, with the substrate
that produced it. Numbers are the corpus's own and retain its scope limits.

| Recorded cost | Where | Cause |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adding `editedAt: time ?` (44 bytes × 244 retained copies = 10,736) pushed `messages.searchAll` to 4,199,688 closure bytes against 4,194,304; the builder shrank a page from 20 to 19 | LLP 1018.008.008; 0.0.21 day-two diary | **Static byte pricing.** Closure bytes are a bound on what a receipt could ship; the product paid for the bound, not the work |
| The 1,000-membership digest fold certifies at 9,922,698 heap and 8,691,704 closure bytes and is refused; the recipient-keyed form is admitted at 2,733,253 / 1,848,418 | LLP 1025 §3 | **Static byte pricing** (heap = steps × 32 + closure scratch). A runtime meter would have run the fold and reported actual bytes |
| A message page is 50, not 200, because a row "prices at 16,016 bytes" and 200 exceed the 1 MiB output ceiling | LLP 1012 §3 | **Static byte pricing** of the worst-case row width |
| A per-recipient search projection is refused at 20,863,641 reserved steps against 1,000,000 | LLP 1024 §1 | **Static step pricing** of a derivation the engine would perform incrementally |
| Full acquisition of 10,008 messages: 67.4 s → 53.6 s → 32.6 s → 28.4 s against a 2–3 s objective; 201 requests, 200 acknowledgement mutations, 612 IndexedDB transactions; PowerSync reports 10,000 rows in 0.36 s native and 2.5 s on web | LLP 1018.008.007; LLP 1018.008.009 §4 | **Result cache, not replica.** Every 50-row page is a certified query, a receipt, a durable acknowledgement mutation, and a store swap |
| Adding a count to a thread makes the composite `partial` offline whenever count authority is missing, so the template declares an independent `history` query and `withHistory` bindings; 27 files / 1,718 lines → 28 / 1,751 after the fix | LLP 1018.008 F2; LLP 1018.008.009 §2 | **Result cache.** The count is not a fact the receipt covers, so the client cannot compose locally |
| `shared/thread.ts` describes table identity, row selection, order, create membership, draft fields and mutation targets a second time | LLP 1018.008 F2 | **Result cache.** Items must be reconstructed from answers because there is no local table to read |
| Sending stalled with 85 IDs remaining against a certified 352-ID slice on an 8,192-ID grant; recovery needed a "Sync now" action | LLP 1018.008 F1 | **Result cache + engine-minted IDs.** Prediction needs server ids before commit, so ids are granted in slices |
| A follower browser tab writes through a durable inbox row; the SDK carries `forwarding`, `writer-unresponsive`, `inbox-full`, `follower` states | LLP 1018.004 | **Result cache.** One writer per partition image; other tabs cannot mutate a receipt-proven image |
| Global search over 1,000 memberships costs ~1,010 HTTP requests through a client N-way merge | LLP 1024 §2 | **Result cache** (no local index over the partition) + **static pricing** (the server-side projection is refused) |
| The complete local runtime grew 7.8 KB → 89,843 gzip bytes; the ceiling was raised 34,000 → 50,000 → 66,000 → 82,000 → 82,500 → 84,000 → 85,000 → 90,000 | LLP 1000 §"Revised"; `rules/NOT-DOING.md` §Moves; LLP 1018.008 §10 | **Result cache.** The inputs list of `metrics/twin.jsonl` is coverage spans, extent facts, held directories, posting trees, fact integrity, swap journals — receipt machinery, not the interpreter |
| `Snapshot` carries `data, next, partial, receipt{state, source, seq, next, reason, rule, sites}, freshness{live, staleSince, connection}, local{predicted, retained, caughtUp, watermark}, refusal, status, reason` | LLP 1018.003 §1 | Both. Every field is a true fact about the mechanism; almost none is a fact about the application |
| `Schema::rule_hash` binds every queued write to the complete schema; an optional column on the subject table invalidates unrelated evidence | LLP 1018.002; LLP 1018.008.008 | **Result cache.** Evidence identity exists because held answers must be re-authorized; a replica re-runs the mutation at the server and needs no evidence identity |

Against that, what scored: on the 2026-09-05 three-scorer Clique run
Snapback 2 beat Convex on authorization (82 vs 79) and offline (81 vs 44)
and lost on queries and live (60 vs 85), client integration (59 vs 79),
setup and docs (55 vs 79), diagnostics and loop (66 vs 77), operations (53
vs 76) and scale (62 vs 80); overall 66 vs 81. The later single-lane 0.0.21
and 0.0.25 scores of 82 and 85 are real improvement and not a three-scorer
comparison. The wins are the core; the losses are composition, discovery,
scale and operations — the substrates and what sits on them.

LLP 2000 §2 summarises this as "costs arise where one layer must reconstruct
another layer's facts." True, and the layer doing the reconstructing is the
client, because it was designed as a cache that may not compute. Name the
cause and the remedy follows.

## 3. Where LLP 2000 is right, and should not be diluted

- **The whole application is the unit.** §3's four properties (composition,
  locality, continuity, performance) are the right acceptance vocabulary.
  Keep them as the headings of the scenario suite.
- **Ownership before abstraction.** §5's table is the best paragraph in the
  document. §8 below turns its fourth row into a fixed vocabulary.
- **Composed reads must not fabricate.** Unknown totals stay unknown; revoked
  rows do not linger. Both flips below preserve this; they change what is
  *known*, not what is *claimed*.
- **Writes have one lifecycle with durable admission distinct from commit
  and stable operation identity.** Keep LLP 1018.004's three outcomes.
- **Rust and SQLite.** Yes, and the engine's transaction, interpreter, rule
  evaluation and successor logic are the pieces to copy first (§5).
- **Prototype the experience before the core.** Yes — and §8 makes the
  prototype's API signatures binding on the implementation, not the reverse.
- **Deterministic simulation early**, citing TigerBeetle. This is the single
  most important sentence in LLP 2000 and §11 makes it the primary harness.
- **A real deployment and a native client early.** Yes; §11 puts the native
  client in slice one.
- **Parallel, fenced, 2000-series, promotion is the author's later call.** Yes.

## 4. Where LLP 2000 is wrong or leaves the wrong things open

### 4.1 It reopens the language and leaves the bound model open; invert that

§6 asks to "evaluate Quarry" and "evaluate any alternative against the same
contracts." The one-language decision took thirty-seven paper applications,
a three-family panel, a spelling spike and an author ruling (LLP
0329.014.004 §9, LLP 1004, LLP 1007 §4b) to close, and the shipped result is
the most readable artifact in Snapback 2: the chat schema is 129 lines and a
query is three to six. Reopening the language will consume the first month.
What *should* be reopened is the bound model under it, which §6 phrases as a
comparison to "concentrate early investigation on." Half of the Quarry
refusals a builder meets are the certifier's byte and step prices wearing
Quarry syntax; the `fn`/`proc` split and the page-to-list gap (LLP 1018.008
F4) are bounded-composition restrictions the pricing model forced. Decide
the bound model (§6), keep the language, and re-derive its restrictions.

### 4.2 The first slice is at the scale where Snapback 2 looked elegant

§10 proposes "a private conversation with paginated messages and an unread
count," "two principals and two clients." Snapback 2 passed that slice in
its first week. It broke at the hub: 1,000 memberships refused the digest
fold, made global search cost a thousand requests, and refused the
envelopes builders first wrote (LLPs 1023, 1024, 1025). Every one of those
was discovered by a cold builder on a published package weeks after the
architecture was fixed. The first slice must include the hub principal from
day one (§11).

### 4.3 It investigates the client data model; the field has answered

§6 says "test a canonical local store with deliberate derived indexes,
durable pending operations, and explicit coverage and authorization
knowledge." That sentence describes a replica; the field has shipped it
under several names (buckets, mutators and a change stream, per-device
queues), Snapback 2's own LLP 1012 §6 reaches it by the hard road ("the
coverage rule that makes everything on the phone exact" is a fully drained
partition), and LLP 1019's `deliver` already computes the audience the
engine must maintain. Decide it (§7) and spend the investigation budget on
the two things the field has *not* settled for this design: partition
inversion of Quarry rules, and horizons at hub scale.

### 4.4 "Builder, human or agent" is two audiences

§3's first row conflates them. Agents need structured refusals with a
pasted rewrite, a one-screen grammar, and a served guide keyed by symbol.
Humans need a language server, a formatter, a dev overlay that shows the
live data and the queries watching it, and error text that reads as a
sentence. Snapback 2 measured only the agent (correction loops,
time-to-green) and shipped no editor support. "Beautiful" for the human
builder is mostly tooling, and it is cheap if it is in slice one and
expensive if it is retrofitted onto a parser that was never an LSP.

### 4.5 It inherits Snapback 2's process shape

§9 and §11 are careful about *release* apparatus and silent about *design*
apparatus. The Snapback 2 record is seventy-one LLPs numbered 1000 and up
in fourteen days, over two thousand commits on `main`, four-to-six-model
panels per RFC, three fix rounds per lane, and a runtime ceiling raised
seven times under a rule that says "a feature that does not fit is descoped
or paid for by deletion." The rigor was right for authorization and custody
and is why the core is sound; the cycle from "a builder felt friction" to
"the friction is gone" is a week and adds mechanism each time. §12 proposes
a smaller shape.

## 5. The proposal in three tables

### 5.1 Keep verbatim (copy the code where it is clean)

| Piece | Where it lives today | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Rule language; uniformity and hoist analysis; `decided_true`; `E_STRUCTURAL_*`; hidden-versus-absent; projections with a declared audience | LLP 1001 §3; `certificate*.rs` shape half | The reason authorization scores 82–93. Static, sound, and legible |
| One data language, Quarry: parser, typer, direct typed binding, `--` comments, `require … else CODE`, `shape`, `merge`, `walk`, `chunk` | LLP 1004, LLP 1010, LLP 1011, LLP 1018.000; `packages/compiler/src/quarry/` | Readable by humans, learnable by agents in one to three refusals |
| The value model and its 324-case conformance corpus; canonical hashing; opaque cursors | `corpus/`; LLP 1011 | The contract any second interpreter is held to |
| Typed read and write states; no silent state; the refusal envelope `{code, family, site, message, rewrite, guide}`; ~20 families | LLP 1001 §3; LLP 1002 §4 | The most praised property across 125 Snapback 1 diaries and every Snapback 2 run |
| Successor classifier: widening is additive, enum removal is checked narrowing, projection ownership is exact; `check --cost`; `guide successor` | LLP 1020 Part A | Day two, done right once |
| `deliver`: engine-computed audience, tombstones to departed members, `bootstrap`, `with receipts` | LLP 1019 | Generalizes into `sync` (§7); the hardest part is already built |
| `schedule`, `job`, `chunk`, rehearsal `--dry-run`, the system owner seat | LLP 1016 | Background work done right once |
| Personas `--as`; authentication always on; `serve` refuses personas | LLP 1002 §1 | Closed Snapback 1's identity friction |
| `init`, `dev` (attach-idempotent), `check` (< 1 s), `doctor` (36 ms), `run`, `test` with a receipt, `why`, `trace`, `guide <keyword>` cards; the measured loop | LLP 1002; `scripts/measure.mjs` | The loop is where the scoreboard moves (LLP 0329.016 §2) |
| H0 control plane: `deploy` to a directory, release ledger, snapshots, `rollback`, `secret`, token admission | LLP 1015 §7 | Static stability from day one |
| Channels and `connected` | LLP 1001.000 | Ephemeral state done right once |
| Assets: typed upload intent, `once`, the media helper | LLP 1006 | The upload path is online anyway; keep it |
| Unicode word-prefix index design | LLP 1012 §"Indexed held search" | Runs over the replica instead of over held ranges |

### 5.2 Flip (decide, do not investigate)

| Snapback 2 | Snapback 3 | Section |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The certificate prices examined, index entries, rule probes, steps, heap, output, closure and write rows, and refuses at `check` | The certificate certifies read-site shape, uniformity, completeness class and prediction eligibility; a deterministic runtime meter enforces work with `capped` reads and aborted writes that name actual work | §6 |
| The client is a result cache: per-query receipts, coverage, halos, validators, per-table `retain` words, `drain`, grants and ID slices | The client is a replica of the viewer's declared partitions with a per-partition watermark; queries run locally over it; ids are client-minted | §7 |
| Client vocabulary derived from the mechanism, then a "plain tier" layered on | Client vocabulary fixed at the experience prototype and binding on the mechanism | §8 |
| TypeScript twin on every client (no WASM, no native module) | Rebuild the twin over the replica and measure; Rust core as a native module where SQLite is already native | §9 |
| Effects in an embedded engine with a subset library | Measure the reference apps' effect corpus on the builder's own Node, an embedded engine, and a hosted isolate before choosing | §10 |
| First slice: two principals, one conversation | First slice: the hub fixture; day-two change designed first; native in slice one | §11 |
| Verification: 39,000 lines of tests, real-owner jsdom, Chromium matrices, simulator witnesses | A deterministic simulator as the primary harness; real-browser and device witnesses only at boundaries the simulator cannot reach | §11 |
| Many concurrent sessions on the core; ceilings raised to fit | One owner on the core for slice one; budgets anchored to a competitor's measured number | §12 |

### 5.3 Delete (do not carry into `snapback3/`)

Closure, heap, output and step certification and their diagnostics; range
receipts, validators, halos and absence evidence; coverage spans, extent
facts, held directories, fact integrity, swap journals; the `drain`
protocol and its watermark cursor; grants, ID slices, `E_OFFLINE_ID_BLOCK`;
follower-tab inbox rows and their four states; `defineItems`,
`withHistory`, `mutationTarget`; `receipt.source`, `local.caughtUp`,
`local.watermark`, `partial` reason codes; `retain` as a receipt rule (the
two words survive as a client-side leave policy, §7.2); the 90,000-byte
twin as it stands.

## 6. Flip 1: certify authorization statically, meter resources at runtime

**What stays static.** Every read site's table, index, prefix template,
order and `take`; whether the read rule is provably uniform over the
scanned prefix (`decided_true`, hoisting); the completeness class
(`complete | capped | maintained`); the structural-fact refusals (a count,
a page length, a `hasMore`, a loop tally over a non-uniform scan is not a
fact about the resource); disclosures; which mutations are locally
predictable (§7.3). All of this is authorization and semantics. It is what
makes a Snapback program safe to admit to every client, and it is what
Convex, Supabase and PocketBase do not have.

**What becomes dynamic.** Rows examined, index entries touched, rule probes,
interpreter steps, transaction heap, output bytes and rows written are
charged by a deterministic meter as the program runs, on the server and in
the client interpreter alike. The ceilings keep their names and roughly
their values. Exceeding one has a typed outcome per kind:

- A **query** returns `capped` at the meter with no continuation past the
  cut, exactly as LLP 1001 §3's "empty capped page is the terminal drain
  state" already behaves for a hidden run. Reads never abort.
- A **mutation** aborts and rolls back with `E_BOUND` in family `bound`,
  naming the *actual* meter, the actual row and site, and the same rewrite
  families Snapback 2 pastes today (a smaller page, a maintained aggregate,
  a `chunk` in a job).
- A **job** is chunked and resumable by construction (LLP 1016) and reports
  measured cost per chunk.

**What `check` and `test` say instead of refusing.** `check --cost` prints
the *structural* cost class from the static shape — constant, one page,
lanes × page, fan-out × page — with the sites that produce it. `test` runs
every operation against the scale fixture (§11) and prints *measured*
meters beside the ceilings. A builder learns that a query examines 5,113
rows on the hub principal by reading a number, not by being refused on a
bound that assumed the widest row 244 times.

**Why this is sound.** The Snapback 2 corpus already contains the argument.
"A denied candidate consumes the certified examined and rule-probe budgets,
never `take`"; a hoisted-false range "halts after the single determination
without fetching or charging the hidden population row by row"; a
non-uniform scan cut by the budget returns "the visible rows found so far
and the opaque `Capped` label" (LLP 1001 §3). A runtime meter applies the
same policy at the same points, so the hidden-population oracle is
identical to today's: none where the rule hoists, and an opaque cap where
it does not. Padded execution for secrecy-sensitive programs (LLP
0329.014.004 §2.2 L) remains a declared program property enforced by the
meter reporting the bound instead of the actual.

**What it dissolves.** Every row of §2 marked "static pricing"; the loop-
carried-value pricing problem (LLP 1020); the `fn`/`proc` split and the
page-to-list gap, which existed to keep collections priceable; seven
diagnostic subfamilies about byte products; the `certificate/search/closure.rs`
and `cap.rs` estimators; the argument about whether 4 MiB is the right
closure ceiling (there is no closure).

**What it costs.** A program can ship that is fine on the seed and expensive
on the hub, and the failure surfaces at runtime for that user. The
mitigations are the scale fixture in `test`, the structural cost class in
`check`, and the meter as backstop — which is the posture of every backend
Snapback is compared against, and those backends score 80–85 on queries.
The DoS argument is unchanged: no request does more than the meter allows.

## 7. Flip 2: the viewer's partition is the unit of sync

### 7.1 The declaration

A table is either **synced** — the engine maintains, per principal, the set
of its rows that principal may read, as a change log — or it is
**online-only**. The word goes on the table, where LLP 0329 said capability
must be legible:

```q
table messages:
  conversationId: conversations
  authorId: principal
  body: text <=4000 ?
  at: time
  by byConvTime: conversationId, at, id
  read <- exists memberships[.conversationId, viewer]
  sync to memberships[.conversationId] last 500 by byConvTime
```

`sync to memberships[.conversationId]` is LLP 1019's `deliver to
memberships[.conversationId]` generalized: the engine inverts the read rule
into an audience it can maintain (a membership row's insert adds a
conversation's rows to a principal's partition by `bootstrap`; its delete
emits one scope tombstone). `last 500 by byConvTime` is the **horizon**: the
partition holds at most that many rows per group, and older rows are
online-only. A table whose rule the engine cannot invert has no `sync`
line and is online-only; `check` says which rule term prevented it and
pastes the shape that would (an equality prefix on a principal or a key).
A `maintain` whose group key is in a synced table is delivered into the
partition as a value row, so a count is a local fact and the composite of
§2 row 6 needs no fallback.

### 7.2 What the client does with it

The client holds, per principal, one SQLite-shaped store of synced rows,
indexed as the schema declares; a per-partition **watermark** (one sequence
number); a durable **outbox** of mutations with client-minted ids; and the
interpreter. A query is a Quarry program run locally over the store. It is
`complete` inside the horizon and `partial` beyond it or at an online-only
site — the same honest states as today, with one reason each. A mounted
query is reactive: every applied batch re-runs the queries whose read sites
overlap the batch's tables (the trace-indexed subscription logic from
`live.rs`, moved to the client). A mutation runs locally first if it is
predictable (§7.3), producing `pending` rows with the ids it minted; then
it is sent; the server's commit arrives in the partition stream and
replaces the prediction.

Acquisition is the partition stream: one request per partition from the
watermark, the response chunked at a fixed byte size, each chunk applied in
one store transaction, the watermark advanced with it. There is no per-page
query, no receipt, no acknowledgement mutation. Delivery receipts (`with
receipts`) become one acknowledgement per stream position, which LLP 1019
already derives. Multi-tab is an SDK concern: one tab owns the store under
a Web Lock and the others read it through a shared worker; no application
sees `forwarding`.

Revocation: a scope tombstone removes the group's rows from the store, or
keeps them under the table's leave policy. LLP 1012 §5's two words survive
with their exact product meaning — `until-revoked` (the default: rows go)
and `delivered-history` (an expelled member keeps what was delivered, and
gets nothing new) — enforced by the client on the tombstone, not by a
receipt rule. A body deletion is an ordinary row tombstone. Neither needs
hidden-versus-absent reasoning on the client, because the client never
held a row it could not read.

### 7.3 Prediction, ids, generations

A mutation is **predictable** when every read it performs is over synced
tables and its writes touch synced tables; this is decidable at `check`
from the read-site shape and visible at the authoring site. A read outside
the partition (a cap over rows the viewer cannot see, an online-only table)
makes the mutation `pending` without a local row, never a fabricated one.
Prediction validity is settled at commit by the server, exactly as today.

Ids are **client-minted** (a time-ordered 128-bit form) for client inserts
and engine-minted for server writes; `new` on the client mints locally.
This deletes grants and slices. The cost is sixteen bytes per id and no
dense sequence, which nothing in the reference applications needs.

A partition stream is bound to a **schema generation**. An additive
successor applies in place; a narrowing successor re-bootstraps the
affected tables; a queued write carries the generation it was authored
under and is judged by LLP 1020's widening rules at the server. There is no
evidence identity to scope (§2 last row), because no held answer needs
re-authorizing — the partition is re-derived by the engine.

### 7.4 What it costs, honestly

- **Device storage is the partition, not what was looked at.** Bounded by
  horizons; this is what a local-first user expects and what the hub
  fixture must size. The hub principal's 1,000 conversations at `last 500`
  is an upper bound of 500,000 rows, so the horizon will be time- or
  count-based per app; the first slice measures it.
- **Server write amplification** for partition maintenance is real and is
  the price LLP 1019 already pays for `deliver`. Joining a large group is a
  bootstrap stream, not a write; leaving is one tombstone.
- **Online-only tables are a visible boundary.** A query mixing synced and
  online-only tables is `partial` offline at the online-only site. This is
  the honest form of Snapback 1's retention maze (LLP 0329 §"The
  mechanism") with the answer written on the table.
- **Two delivery modes, one language.** A client without a local store (an
  anonymous public feed, a dashboard) subscribes to server-evaluated live
  queries exactly as Snapback 2 does today. A client with a store subscribes
  to its partitions and evaluates locally. Same programs, same interpreter,
  different transport, chosen by the app's client configuration.
- **The security argument changes shape and keeps its strength.** Snapback
  2: "the client never computes a fact the receipt did not cover." Snapback
  3: "the client only holds rows in its partition, and the partition is its
  read authority, computed by the engine." Same guarantee, one mechanism.

### 7.5 What it dissolves

Every row of §2 marked "result cache." Concretely: `withHistory`,
`defineItems`, `mutationTarget`, `drain`, grants, ID slices, follower
inbox rows, receipts, coverage, extents, halos, validators, evidence
identity, and the fourteen twin inputs that are not the interpreter. The
global search of LLP 1024 becomes one local query over the partition's
word-prefix index within the horizon plus one server query beyond it. The
digest of LLP 1025 is a server job over server data, which flip 1 lets run.

## 8. The client vocabulary, fixed before the mechanism

LLP 2000 §4's experience prototype should produce, and §11's evaluation
should hold the implementation to, the complete set of states a screen can
show. It fits on an index card, and the mechanism must fit the card:

```ts
// A read. `complete` is false beyond the horizon or at an online-only site.
type Read<T> = { data: T; complete: boolean; fresh: boolean; since?: number; next?: Cursor }
            | { loading: true } | { denied: Refusal };
// A row this device changed and the server has not yet confirmed.
row.pending: boolean;
// A write. Three outcomes, all terminal or durable (LLP 1018.004).
type Write = { state: "pending" } | { state: "sent"; seq: number } | { state: "failed"; why: Refusal };
// The app.
type Link = "online" | "offline" | "signed-out";
```

Everything Snapback 2's `Snapshot` carries beyond this is an answer to
`why`, and `why` is where it belongs. The test is not "can the mechanism
express its facts" but "does a screen need any word not on the card." The
chat `Thread` should be `useQuery` plus JSX; the `Composer` should be
`useMutation` plus a form; `shared/thread.ts` and `shared/status.ts`
should not exist.

## 9. One interpreter, three hosts — re-measure with today's numbers

Snapback 2 ruled "never a native module" and "no WASM anywhere" on a
measurement: a 7.8 KB TypeScript twin against a 109 KB read-only Rust/WASM
subset (LLP 0329.014.004 §7). The twin now measures 89,843 gzip bytes and
the public client 142,989, so the ruling's premise is gone, and LLP
0329.014.004 §2.2 P already concedes a device-plane runtime for
device-authoritative partitions.

Under flip 2 the twin is the interpreter, the value model, the index and a
store adapter — roughly what the spike measured. So the recommendation is
not "switch to WASM"; it is: **rebuild the twin over the replica, measure
it, and set its budget from a competitor's shipped client** (§12). If it
lands near 20 KB, TypeScript stays on web. On Expo, SQLite is already
native (the carrier is `expo-sqlite`), so the Rust core as a native module
is the likely winner there and removes the two-interpreter conformance
obligation on the platform where process death and media make it hardest
to hold. Web keeps the corpus as its contract. Two client **tiers** — an
online-only client with no store and no interpreter, and a local-first
client with both — are one language and one server, and the tier is chosen
in the app's client configuration.

## 10. Effects: measure the sandbox against the builder's Node

Snapback 2 embeds Ibex 2 over Hermes for effects: a 7.9 MB binary against
4.5 MB without, a subset standard library, no imported helpers, "Node/npm
SDK compatibility is explicitly not promised," and a steady stream of
tickets (dropped fetch headers, string subset, `Intl` declarations exceeding
the runtime). LLP 2000 §6 asks to "test ordinary imported helpers and
representative libraries before settling the effects runtime" and says "a
custom environment must repay those costs." Agreed, with the prior stated:
the reference applications need a push notification, an email, a webhook
and an image resize, and every one of those is easiest in the builder's own
Node with an npm SDK. The outbox contract — durable job identity,
idempotency key, at-least-once, `sent_unknown` — is enforced by the
**server** and does not depend on the sandbox. Grants (allowed origins) can
be enforced by routing the worker's `fetch` through the owner. What an
embedded engine buys is tenant isolation and deterministic replay; the
former belongs to the hosted target's process model and the latter to the
outbox. The strategic weight of Ibex is the author's to place; this
document only records that the record's effect friction is the subset, not
the model.

## 11. The first slice, revised

LLP 2000 §10's stages are right. Their content changes in five ways.

1. **The fixture is the hub.** The Clique generator's `u0001` (100 groups,
   900 DMs, 1,000 memberships), `u0500` (500 follows), and 100,000 messages
   are the cold-acquisition dataset from day one, not a later scale step.
   "Search everything," "unread across everything," and the period digest
   are in the first scenario set, before media.
2. **Day two before day one.** Before the first screen: what identifies a
   generation; what a queued write is bound to; what a client holding a
   partition does on an additive, a widening, a narrowing and a rule change;
   what a rename does. The scenario suite gains "change a rule while a
   write is queued" and "rename a column with an old client attached."
3. **Native in slice one.** The Expo client's physics (no streaming
   `Response` body, `AVPlayer` refusing data URLs, process death mid-write)
   decide §9, so they cannot wait for slice three.
4. **Floors before targets.** Before building the sync path, measure and
   record: one gzipped response of 10,000 rows; one IndexedDB transaction
   writing 10,000 rows; one `expo-sqlite` bulk insert of 10,000 rows; a
   local query over 100,000 rows in the TypeScript interpreter and in
   SQLite; a 50-member send's partition fan-out on the server. The design
   target is within 2× of the floor. Snapback 2 spent two weeks moving 67 s
   to 28 s on a design whose floor was a second.
5. **The simulator is the harness.** One deterministic program under a
   seed: a single-threaded scheduler, a virtual clock, in-process server and
   N clients, and fault injection — drop, reorder, duplicate and delay
   messages; fail `fsync`; kill a process at any await; skew a clock. Quarry
   programs are already deterministic, so any failure replays from its
   seed. The invariants it checks on every commit: no silent state; a row
   revoked at sequence S never renders after S; a write with a given id
   applies at most once; every prediction converges to the server's state
   or a typed conflict; every screen state is on the card; watermarks are
   monotone; after faults stop, every client converges. Real-browser and
   device witnesses cover only what the simulator cannot: IndexedDB and Web
   Lock semantics, iOS process death, the React Native stream body, media
   playback. Snapback 2 carries 38,815 lines of tests; the simulator plus a
   dozen witnesses should replace most of them.

The slice's observable outcomes in LLP 2000 §10 stand, with two additions:
(6) the hub principal's first useful inbox renders within the §7 budget
while acquisition runs, and global search returns within one round trip;
(7) the loop bar — a fresh agent, the published package, reduced Clique
M1–M3, under 1,200 s with at most one Snapback-attributed correction — is
run in the slice's first week and weekly after, on the same harness and
scorers as Snapback 2, with Convex as the control. Snapback 2 never met it
at 0.0.21 and has not re-measured it in five releases.

## 12. Building Snapback 3 without rebuilding Snapback 2's process

- **One owner on the core for slice one.** LLP 2000 §8 asks for one person
  responsible for coherence. Extend it: one session at a time changes the
  engine, the store and the client contract until the slice runs; fan out
  only for measurements, reviews and the reference apps. Concurrency was
  Snapback 2's velocity and its incoherence.
- **Budgets anchored to competitors, held by deletion.** The online-tier
  client's gzip size is measured against Convex's shipped React client; the
  local-first tier's against PowerSync's web client; the server binary
  against PocketBase. Raising a budget then means writing "we are now
  larger than X," which is harder to do quietly than moving 85,000 to
  90,000. The loop budgets stay Snapback 2's: `check` under a second,
  `doctor` under half, the blocking suite under a minute.
- **No ceiling is raised in slice one.** If a feature does not fit, the
  design changes or the feature waits. This rule already exists in
  `rules/NOT-DOING.md`; Snapback 3 should be the first place it holds.
- **One design document, updated in place; one experiment journal,
  append-only.** LLP 2000 says this in §9. The journal replaces the Revised
  header as a change log. Panels are for authorization and custody
  mechanisms; everything else is judged by the simulator, the scenario
  suite and the weekly Clique run.
- **The scenario suite is the spec.** LLP 2000 §4's six scenarios,
  executable, on the hub fixture, in the simulator, are what "done" means.
  A document with no scenario behind it is a proposal (which is
  `rules/RULES.md`'s rule already).
- **The human builder is measured too.** Time for a person to add a field
  and see it in the editor, the overlay and the running app; whether the
  language server catches the refusal before `check` does; whether a
  screen's states are explained in a sentence.

## 13. Risks, and what would falsify this

| Claim | Falsified if |
| --- | --- |
| Partition inversion covers the reference apps | The messenger or the field-work app needs a synced table whose read rule the engine cannot invert and cannot be restructured to a key prefix without changing the product |
| The horizon keeps the hub's partition affordable | The hub principal's partition at a product-honest horizon exceeds the device budget or the acquisition budget by more than 2× the measured floor |
| Runtime metering does not regress disclosure | The simulator finds an observable difference between a static ceiling cut and a metered cut on any non-uniform scan |
| The twin over a replica is small | It measures above the competitor-anchored budget after the store adapter and index are in, and WASM measures larger still |
| Client-minted ids are enough | A reference application needs a dense engine sequence for a client insert |
| The simulator replaces the matrix | A class of defect the real-browser witnesses catch repeatedly that the simulator's fault model cannot express |
| One owner is faster than the fleet | Slice one does not run the hub fixture within four weeks of the experience prototype |

If the first slice cannot reach the loop bar, cannot acquire the hub
fixture within 2× of its measured floor, or cannot hold the client under
its competitor-anchored budget, the thesis is wrong and Snapback 2 should
receive whichever pieces (the meter, the partition stream, the simulator)
proved out on their own.

## 14. Open questions for the author

1. **Is `sync` a per-table word, or does every table with an invertible
   rule sync by default with an explicit `online-only`?** Default-sync is
   the local-first posture; explicit is the legible one. Recommendation:
   explicit, because the horizon has to be chosen anyway.
2. **What is the default horizon?** A count per group (`last 500`), a time
   window (`last 30d`), or both? Recommendation: both allowed, count
   required.
3. **Does the online-only tier ship in slice one?** It is small and it is
   what an anonymous feed needs. Recommendation: yes, because it is the
   control for every "local-first made this slower" measurement.
4. **Rust core as a native module on Expo — decide now or after §9's
   measurement?** Recommendation: after, but with the measurement in slice
   one's first week.
5. **Effects host.** Builder's Node, embedded engine, hosted isolate — or
   all three behind one contract? Recommendation: measure the four
   reference effects on each; the strategic question about Ibex is yours.
6. **Which client-minted id form?** A time-ordered 128-bit form is the field
   default; the engine's namespace rule (LLP 1006 §9a) must accept it.
7. **Does LLP 2000 absorb this document, or stand beside it?** The parent's
   §6 and §10 would change; the rest would not.

## Confidence

- **High** that §2's causal mapping is right: every row cites a corpus
  document that names the mechanism, and the elegance assessments (LLPs
  1018.008, 1018.008.009) reach the same two substrates without naming them.
- **High** that flip 1 preserves the disclosure posture, from LLP 1001 §3's
  own text; **medium** on how much builder-visible cost it removes, because
  some refusals are honest scale problems that a meter only relocates.
- **Medium-high** on flip 2: the shape is field-proven and Snapback 2's
  `deliver` and drained-partition coverage already instantiate it; the
  unproven part is partition inversion of arbitrary Quarry rules and the
  horizon at hub scale, which §11 measures first.
- **Medium** on §9 and §10; both are "measure with today's numbers," not
  rulings.
- **High** that the first slice must include the hub and the day-two
  change; the record is unambiguous about where Snapback 2 broke.
- **Medium** on §12; process claims are harder to falsify than mechanisms.

## Non-claims

- This document accepts nothing on the author's behalf and commissions no
  code. It names no implementer and no date; under `rules/RULES.md` it is a
  proposal until a slice runs.
- It does not re-decide the language, the loop verbs, personas, or the
  rule language; it cites where each was decided.
- It changes no Snapback 2 status, scope, ceiling or gate, and proposes no
  edit to `rules/NOT-DOING.md` outside a future `snapback3/` fence.
- Its numbers are the corpus's own and keep their original scope; the
  competitor sizes it names as anchors are to be measured, not assumed.
# What Snapback 4 Does Not Do

Longer than the doing-list on purpose. Moving something off it means writing one
line naming what it unblocks and taking something off the doing-list in the same
change. Nothing comes off during slice one (LLP 3000 §3: the clock is four weeks).

## The bars

The bakeoff gates in [LLP 3000 §3](../llp/3000-snapback4.explainer.md) are the
bars: the loop bar, hub acquisition within 2× the measured floor, client bytes
under the competitor-anchored budgets, the six scenarios green in the simulator
on the hub fixture. Everything not required to pass them does not exist yet.

## Not building

- **A second authoring language.** Quarry, one spelling, copied from Snapback 2 (LLP 1004, LLP 1007 §4b). TypeScript is for seeds, journeys, effect bodies and the client.
- **Byte- and step-priced certification.** No closure, heap, output or step ceilings at `check`; the certificate certifies shape, uniformity, completeness class and prediction eligibility. Resources are metered at runtime (LLP 2000.000 §6).
- **A per-query result cache.** No range receipts, validators, halos, absence evidence, coverage spans, extent facts, held directories, fact integrity or swap journals. The client replicates declared partitions with one watermark each (LLP 2000.000 §7).
- **Engine-minted ids for client inserts, grants, ID slices.** Ids are client-minted, time-ordered, 128-bit; the engine mints for its own writes.
- **The `drain` protocol, follower-tab inbox rows, `defineItems`, `withHistory`, `mutationTarget`, `receipt.source`, `local.caughtUp`, `partial` reason codes.** The card (`packages/snapback4/src/contract.ts`) is the whole client vocabulary; `why` carries the rest.
- **`retain` as a receipt rule.** `until-revoked` and `delivered-history` survive as the client's leave policy on a scope tombstone.
- **JavaScript in the data plane.** Queries and mutations run in the Rust interpreter inside the SQLite transaction, and in the same interpreter on the client (host decided at week 3 by measurement, LLP 2000.000 §9). A *job* may be the app's own Rust, hosted as wasm through the interpreter's operations under the job's meter (LLP 3000.005, the Moves line below); a query or mutation may not.
- **An embedded JS engine for effects.** The data plane never calls out. `emit` writes the effect durably in the mutation's commit and an external worker holding a service token claims, performs and settles it over the wire (`snapback4/worker`); the four reference effects run on the builder's Node (LLP 2000.000 §10, decided 2026-09-13).
- **Committed generated code; an auth mode; a supervised bundler.** As Snapback 2 (`rules/NOT-DOING.md`): types by inference, personas not modes, `SNAPBACK_URL` is the client contract.
- **A TypeScript twin of the interpreter.** Retired by the author's ruling of 2026-09-13 (LLP 3000 §7): one implementation, in Rust, on every host — the core's SQLite under the native module, memory under wasm on the web with the host persisting each commit's changes. The web pays 312,924 B gzip before the diet where the twin's tier paid 16,842; the conformance obligation between two interpreters ends. Expo Go runs the online tier.
- **More than one hosted target, an admin console, media transcoding or thumbnails, push delivery itself (a worker's), cron syntax beyond `every N at HH:MM`, vector or relevance-ranked search (word-prefix search is in: `words(..)` and `matching`), federation, multi-region, device-to-device sync, at-rest encryption claims.** After the clock, on evidence.
- **General observability beyond `why`, `trace`, `logs`, `data`, `sql`, server operation latency, and bounded client subscription inspection.** The `sql` verb is read-only inspection of the developer's own store (LLP 3000.007 §8.8), never an application surface. Separately declared application SQL reads are admitted for increment D (LLP 3000.009 §6.2, §14.4; the 2026-09-16 Moves line below), not implemented by this inspection verb.
- **A guide corpus.** The compiler's facts, one language card and keyword cards, as Snapback 2; no freshness dates. Every card's Quarry is compiled by the suite and the language card is run, so a card cannot promise a form the parser refuses.

- **Partial union delivery, two group audiences, a public branch.** A union syncs principal-column branches and at most one group branch, each a whole disjunct of the read rule (LLP 3000.036.000.000); a branch with an extra condition, a second group or a `public` branch is refused with the rewrite, never partially delivered; service grants add no device audience (LLP 3000.007 §20.6). A union does not combine with delivered history, read-through children, synced or row-keyed views, owner keys, timelines, private field owners, acquisition windows or direct/inherited expiry on content or membership scopes.
- **Exact horizon maintenance.** The horizon bounds acquisition (a snapshot, a bootstrap); the log never evicts or back-fills to hold a device's group at exactly N rows. A local read past the acquired horizon by key says `complete: false`.

- **Clock expressions in rules.** A read rule compares with `=` and `!=` only; `.expiresAt > now` remains a mutation `require`. Stored lifetimes use `not before .col` for server-controlled opening and `expire at .col` for expiry. The engine publishes opening commits; devices receive no unopened payload. The supported compositions and explicit exclusions are in LLP 3000.036.003.
- **A jobs framework.** No priorities, worker pools or dead letters: one FIFO of runs, one chunk at a time, a refused chunk parked with its refusal. Actions, effects and scheduled actions carry per-operation retries, deadlines and cancellation, and a record per external step (LLP 3000.009 §5.2; the Moves line below). Missed period boundaries while a server is down fire once on return, not once per missed period.
- **Asset processing.** No transcoding, thumbnails, rungs or HEIC: the bytes are served as uploaded; JPEG, PNG, WebP and MP4 only; image dimensions and optional MP4 duration read from the header; MP4 dimensions are not extracted.
- **Search beyond word prefixes.** One tokenizer (NFC, lowercase, alphanumeric words), one index shape (`scope, words(cols), order.., id`), one read (`matching`): every term a prefix of some word, the first term's words merged as lanes, bounded by the meter in a uniform scope, at most 32 words in an admitted non-uniform scope. No stemming, no ranking by relevance, no phrase or mid-word matching, no per-language folding, no intersection index for the second term (it filters the page).
- **An identity provider.** Guests, password accounts, service seats, and sessions a service mints for accounts the application authenticated itself. No phone, OAuth, magic links or MFA in the engine: an application's worker verifies those and mints the session.
- **No multi-server presence, no presence history or last-seen storage, no broadcast event stream, no connection-bound rows.** `connected[p]` stays server-wide, counting held polls and their 35 s grace; a live query that may read it re-runs in the background about every 5 seconds to stay current (LLP 3000.018). Typing and viewing use short-lived, authorized ephemeral tables in one server process (LLP 3000.017); they never sync or decide durable invariants. A device does not know presence and says so.

## Process not doing

- **2026-09-28 — ordinary mixed search** (LLP 3000.028 §3.3, lane OS round 1).
  Admit the SDK's bounded merge of current server rows and certified Left images
  for the proved single time-ordered word page. This removes application-owned
  online/held selection for that shape. One server call per requested page;
  recomputation pays again from consumed positions. The coordinator shares the
  existing device read allowance. No durable search session, extra synced rows,
  new authority or additional allowance is admitted.

- **2026-09-28 — inferred current-scope word search** (LLP 3000.028 §3.3,
  lane OS). A single bare ordinary word page may derive the exact non-expiring
  membership audience for the existing compact server plan. This removes the
  requirement to repeat that audience predicate in this narrow server query.
  The compiler supplies a scoped words index when absent; it is an ordinary
  schema index, maintained on writes and on devices, with no extra synced rows
  or new authority. The device keeps its original bare scan. Round 0 left ordered composition
  and combined accounting outside its independent subset; round 1 above admits
  that bounded SDK composition. Durable search sessions remain excluded.
- **2026-10-12 — union sync audiences** (LLP 3000.036.000.000, workstream UNION, two blind review rounds). A table syncs to the union of principal-column audiences and one group audience; a device holds a row while any branch admits its viewer. What it unblocks: owner-or-team, buyer-or-seller and owner-or-staff tables offline for every reader, the shape three app farms asked for. Taken off: "one `sync` audience per table". Partial delivery, two groups and a public branch stay excluded (above).
- **2026-09-28 — explicit owner-key coverage** (LLP 3000.028 §3.4, Lane OK commission). A public window may opt into a compiler-owned, horizon-free principal key partition to answer ordinary own-key existence offline. Taken off: the exclusion of narrower platform key distribution beside one public window. General union audiences, full own-row replication and per-query absence caches remain excluded. The projection uses existing metered maintains, adoption and whole-partition publication; existing windows do not expand.
- **2026-09-26 — a live query keeps presence current** (LLP 3000.018). A 0.4.2 probe found an observed `connected[p]` reporting `fresh: true` while stale for over a minute, and a comparative builder polled around it. What it unblocks: online marks and presence-aware lists that stay current. Taken off: "a presence stream" as an exclusion for live queries; a presence API, last-seen storage and multi-server presence stay out. Under the author's standing authorization for reworks that make queries excellent (2026-09-25).

- **No drift registries, inventories, line-pinned diagnostics registries, claim digests or frozen-record gates.** Generated projections are built, not committed.
- **No gate bound to an exact commit; no push gated on a battery.** Five blocking checks in sixty seconds, run before push; everything slower on a schedule with bounded tickets.
- **No ceiling raised in slice one.** Budgets are anchored to a competitor's measured number and recorded once in the journal.
- **No standing panel** except the two blind reviews LLP 3000 decision 4 names (partition inversion; the meter's cut on non-uniform scans). The author explicitly commissioned the one-off LLP 3000.008 panel and disjoint Astra implementation lanes on 2026-09-14; its bounded disposition is recorded in that umbrella and LLP 3000 §7.
- **No new LLP number** without first extending LLP 3000 or LLP 2000.000; no spec without running code (`rules/RULES.md`: only implemented specs bind).
- **No second session on the core** during slice one; lanes take the periphery.
- **No test matrix as the primary harness.** The simulator is; real-browser and device witnesses cover only what it cannot reach.
- **No files over 1,500 lines**, including ones copied from Snapback 2. Split on
  the way in. The one exception is the package's published documents
  (`packages/snapback4/README.md` and its linked references): the rule exists to
  keep a *source* file reviewable, and the 0.2.25 calibration measured the cost of
  applying it to a document — splitting the README was that run's single largest
  scoring loss, because the builder then had four places to look instead of one
  (LLP 3000.007 §20.19). A published document is bounded by whether a builder can
  find one way in, not by a line count; the census that enforces this rule covers
  source, and says so.

## Deliberately worse

- No compatibility with Snapback 1 or Snapback 2 apps, packages or on-disk formats.
- No API stability before the clock; every publish is `0.0.x`.
- Sparse prose documentation; the diagnostics and the recipes carry the load.
- One hosted target, SQLite in the production CLI, one platform binary at a time. A separately enabled Postgres evaluation adapter is not a hosted/deployable target (Moves below).

## Moves

- **2026-10-13 — stored lower lifetimes (`not before`) are commissioned by PAGE2** (LLP 3000.036.003). This unblocks timed disclosure without an application publication job. Per-application jobs that flip a publication flag leave this admitted path; clock expressions in rules, captured lower lifetimes for read-through children, and boundary recounts for maintained data remain excluded. HS owns handshake transport; PAGE2 declares the new semantic capability.

- **2026-10-06 — exact Following history (FH delegated round-1 ruling).**
  Admit a Following-specific server traversal and bounded volatile frontier/replay
  state under LLP 3000.026 §9. The approved design reads exact history online
  from the first page; the SDK will own continuation. The first milestone is internal event metadata
  and the oracle, with no public completeness claim. Taken off: a blanket ban on
  state for this one expiring read. General result caches, resumable queries,
  device traversal, inbox coverage certificates and history-only blocks remain
  excluded. Existing inbox bytes and runtime ceilings remain unchanged.

- **2026-09-26 — server operation latency (LLP 3000.019 §3.7).** Calibration #22
  measured each read once and could not claim repeated p95/degradation testing.
  Unblocks percentile measurements of the application's own load without a
  custom timing harness. Taken off: a latency harness written by the builder;
  general metrics, tracing infrastructure and an admin console remain excluded.

- **2026-09-23 — Quarry states intent moves onto the doing-list** (Charlie:
  "if you need to try making *significant* architectural reworks in order to
  make the data modeling, queries, and scalability be excellent and elegant,
  you can experiment with that"; LLP 3000.013). Unblocks reads that state
  predicates and order, one `view` declaration for derived data, Following
  without caps, jobs and aggregates without author-chosen bounds, an app
  object and an offline web shell. Taken off: index names and author-chosen
  engine numbers on the paved path; the timeline's promotion, pull routes and
  caps; the "Time in rules" refusal is unchanged. Stands: one interpreter, no
  JavaScript in the data plane, no lowering Quarry to SQL, no relevance-ranked
  search.

  work while Workroom continues. Taken off: waiting for Workroom before

Taken off in the same change: the inspection-only exclusion for application SQL and D's pending-author gate. Stands: no SQL writes into managed data, no lowering Quarry to SQL, no SQL in the first Snapback brief cell, and no publication authorization.

Unblocks the online-only brief's invitation, webhook and scheduled journeys without a second application server: work records whose authority is bound at admission, ids bound to their operation and arguments, declared action results, a generated handler context with a durable record per external step, declared routes and webhooks, scheduled actions under service seats, and `serve` supervising the runtime. Taken off in the same change: "A jobs framework" narrows — actions, effects and scheduled actions get per-operation retries, deadlines and cancellation; priorities, worker pools and dead letters stay out — and the actions card's "supervise the worker as an ordinary Node process" leaves. Stands: no JavaScript in the data plane, no identity provider in the engine, and no `ctx.sql`, projections or SQL reads in this increment.

Taken off in the same change: the evaluation prototype's single-adapter framing, in which the only next step was an unbatched store. Stands: Postgres is not a hosted or deployable target; its refusals of assets, jobs, actions and schedules, and `serve`, `deploy` and backup on Postgres, wait for increment C3.

  there is no telemetry transport or server admin console (LLP 3000.023 §10).
# Rules

**Snapback 2 optimizes the edit-to-verified loop — the agent's building an app on it, and ours building it.**

Speed is the constraint. Everything below exists to keep the edit-to-verified loop short
and to stop this repository accreting apparatus faster than anyone can remove it. These
rules bind `snapback2/` and the LLPs numbered 1000 and up; Snapback 1 is baselined, not
exempt, and its numbers may only fall.

Each rule is **[check]** (enforced by `caps`) or **[review]** (enforced by a human).

## Budgets — every budget is a trade, not a limit

- **5 blocking checks, 60s total.** A sixth requires deleting one, in the same PR. **[check]**
- **59 documents in the working set** (`llp/current/`). Today's count. A 60th requires
  archiving one — which is just removing the link. Snapback 2 aims to leave this at 15;
  Snapback 1's links are archived at promotion. **[check]**
- **12 documents in the foundation** (`llp/foundation/`). **[check]**
- **This file: 700 words.** If it grows, something becomes a check or stops being a rule. **[check]**
- **1,500 lines per source file, baseline 204.** Generated files exempt — and generated
  files are built, not committed; so are benchmark run records under `benchmarks/runs/`,
  which are evidence, not source. The baseline is Snapback 1's oversize count on the day
  the rule landed; it may not grow, and `snapback2/` contributes zero to it. **[check]**

## Blocking checks

`caps` · `ref-check` · `snapback2/scripts/test.sh` · `snapback2/scripts/measure.mjs --check` · `check-inventories`

## Loop shape

- **Every check reports all failures in one run.** Fail-fast is banned. **[check]**
- **Nothing blocks on anything slower than 60 seconds.** Slow verification runs on a
  schedule, attributed to the breaking commit, and files a bounded set of issues. **[review]**
- **Fix loops get 3 rounds.** Then stop: escalate to a human or descope. **[review]**

## Time budgets

Tracked every run of `snapback2/scripts/measure.mjs` (appends `snapback2/metrics/loop.jsonl`).
A regression is a P0 with a name on it.

| | budget |
|---|---|
| Incremental rebuild after a one-line edit | 15s |
| Test what you just changed | 30s |
| Blocking gate suite | 60s |
| Full build, warm cache | 5 min |
| **`snapback2 check` on a program (the agent's product latency)** | **1s** |
| **`snapback2 doctor` boot-to-ready** | **0.5s** |

## Scope

- **`NOT-DOING.md` is binding.** Moving something onto the doing-list means writing why
  and taking something off. **[review]**
- **Delete; don't deprecate.** No compat shims or migration paths before promotion. **[review]**
- **Nothing is compiled, transpiled, or generated at runtime that could be built ahead of
  time.** **[review]**
- **A spec needs an implementer and a date, or it isn't written.** **[review]**
- **Only implemented specs bind.** A document with no running code behind it is a
  proposal, and a proposal cannot block a PR or justify a check. **[review]**

## Agents

- **Agents remove apparatus freely and add none.** An agent PR that adds a check, script,
  registry, config file, or design doc needs explicit human approval saying so. **[review]**
- **2026-09-06 session exception:** the user delegated the search implementation
  decisions; one `snapback2-core/build.rs` may generate pinned Unicode tables at
  build time, alongside the existing package build. No new gate, registry,
  runtime generator, or Unicode dependency is authorized by this exception. **[review]**
- **Snapback 2 LLPs are numbered 1000 and up**; `llp-create` must be told. **[review]**
- **Snapback 4 LLPs are numbered 3000 and up** (`snapback4/`, LLP 3000); it adopts these budgets
  and loop shape as its own and keeps its scope in `snapback4/NOT-DOING.md`. **[review]**

## Shared machines

- Warm, named lanes, one per stream of work. Never clone fresh; reuse the worktree.
- **Never `git stash`.** Use a temporary commit.
- Kill only PIDs you recorded at launch. Never by name or pattern.
